Chinese President during the meeting between members of the standing committee of the Political Bureau of the 20th CPC Central Committee. (Photo by Lintao Zhang/Getty Images)

1. The Fifth Plenum ConcludesWhat Happened: The Fifth Plenum concluded on October 29, adopting the Resolution of the Central Committee of the Communist Party of China on Certain Major Issues concerning Persevering with Full and Rigorous Party Self-Governance. It was attended by 162 full Central Committee members, one more than https://click.e.asiasociety.org/?qs=ABt7ImQiOjUwNDUsInQiOjE3NTY5LCJzIjoxMH0ACgAAAAAF37IdlOyCcpoU9H5Zus99NuELsMvbuPbOquWbwUNvRcqQmXM2GEh_fJjVnuVYWc-dHf7ov8xgg2DWUlYcB2dtzMQNvP7lBqx96itB CCA predicted , and 117 alternate members, 13 fewer than https://click.e.asiasociety.org/?qs=ABt7ImQiOjUwNDUsInQiOjE3NTY5LCJzIjoxMH0ACgAAAAAF37IdlO1ZFETxUr-JLvCsdNzwvJ91p8y0FaVC_wyAQQYq40Y6qLYPRCpHuSO6vUVEATtoPtAxkFH6PJVfcVFcOX6-7SqAEmSjwUmj forecas https://click.e.asiasociety.org/?qs=ABt7ImQiOjUwNDUsInQiOjE3NTY5LCJzIjoxMH0ACgAAAAAF37IdlO4ygpXFjbILEvktrRLD7qFebngoskoA73yRb3xTdfBUyDMcxb99N6ReaAkpuCdu12po26uVirS--87IgDOo_RdwLnW5UroG t . More importantly, the plenum announced that the 21st Party Congress would convene in the second half of 2027. Although focused on Party governance, the plenum affirmed the Politburo’s work since the Fourth Plenum across major areas, including politics, economic development, national security, military modernization, Taiwan, and diplomacy. It emphasized strengthening institutional rules governing political discipline, oversight, cadre management, and anti-corruption. On personnel matters, the plenum elected Central Military Commission Vice Chairman Zhang Shengmin to the Politburo and filled ten Central Committee vacancies with alternate members, including Zhejiang Governor Liu Jie and Shanxi Governor Lu Dongliang. It also confirmed earlier disciplinary decisions involving 34 individuals, including the expulsion of Zhang Youxia and 32 others from the Party and the removal of Li Yunze from his Party position.

Why It Matters: The plenum’s sweeping endorsement of Xi’s policies signals an effort to institutionalize his governing model and consolidate his political legacy ahead of the 21st Party Congress. Xi appears determined to strengthen Party discipline and institutional oversight not only to safeguard his leadership and maintain political stability but also to improve policy implementation and economic performance. The plenum called for stricter leadership decision-making procedures, fuller use of macroeconomic policies, and stronger domestic demand to achieve China’s 2026 development targets and secure a strong start to the 15th Five-Year Plan (2026–2030). Yet this approach could present a fundamental challenge: tighter political controls may make officials more risk-averse, potentially undermining the economic dynamism and innovation Beijing seeks to promote. Overall, Xi views political security, Party discipline, and national development as inseparable. Whether tighter institutional control can deliver both political stability and economic vitality remains uncertain. The plenum’s decisions will help shape China’s elite politics and policy direction ahead of the 21st Party Congress in 2027.

By Lobsang Tsering, Senior Research Associate on Chinese Politics, Center for China Analysis

Learn More: Read “ https://click.e.asiasociety.org/?qs=ABt7ImQiOjUwNDUsInQiOjE3NTY5LCJzIjoxMH0ACgAAAAAF37IdlO87OjFFwrXcP-ni-cwNjRyWa7XLLT626cLZen1sNqOdoUzUW3ecq6LIpWGA3tb2QSbd3crmH6FJKuO5IvMZt0tJAp_KvSpb The Limits of Xi’s Control: What to Watch at China’s Fifth Plenum ” by CCA Senior Fellow Neil Thomas, CCA Senior Research Associate Lobsang Tsering, and CCA Research Assistant Shengyu Wang.

2. Treasury Proposes Major Expansion of Outbound Investment RestrictionsWhat Happened: On October 22, Treasury proposed regulations implementing the Comprehensive Outbound Investment National Security (COINS) Act that would significantly expand the U.S. outbound investment security regime. The proposal would extend COINS restrictions beyond China to Iran, North Korea, Russia, and Cuba, while adding high-performance computing, hypersonics, and biotechnology to the technologies already covered, including semiconductors, quantum technologies, and AI. Biotechnology is particularly significant because Treasury is proposing using its COINS Act authority to designate additional technologies, with certain biotechnology investments now subject to outright prohibition. The proposal would also broaden restrictions within the existing semiconductor, quantum, and AI categories.

Why It Matters: The proposal would expand U.S. outbound investment security regime from a relatively narrow, China-focused framework toward a broader economic-security tool restricting U.S. capital and expertise from flowing into sensitive technology sectors across multiple countries of concern. For companies with China exposure, biotechnology and expanded AI restrictions are particularly consequential. For AI, Treasury proposes moving away from a tiered framework toward prohibiting covered U.S. transactions involving foreign entities developing qualifying AI systems. This overall expansion of outbound investment restrictions is likely an effort to increase U.S. leverage in advance of the anticipated Xi-Trump meetings at the APEC and G20 summits. China’s announcement of proposed expanded export controls six days earlier was likely a step in anticipation of further U.S. announcements, such as the COINS announcement, and could serve similar leverage purposes. Treasury’s expansion also comes amid congressional scrutiny of U.S. biotechnology investment in China, including through the proposed Biotech Investment National Security Act. Treasury’s proposal would retain the existing national-interest exemptions and introduce a confidential channel for companies and individuals to submit evidence about whether a foreign entity and its activities falls within the program’s scope.

By Scott Shaw, Non-resident Senior Fellow on Chinese Economy, Center for China Analysis

Learn More: Watch “ https://click.e.asiasociety.org/?qs=ABt7ImQiOjUwNDUsInQiOjE3NTY5LCJzIjoxMH0ACgAAAAAF37IdlPALzvBWAHihL79mX3XSCqYc2jLZZXnbLuhdkGBgtTBEpZ4-DSEkUk4SidbwCylyux_bVoKJMAImA5jS-P9nOhoLY1u0xa5k Are Export Controls Working? Field Notes from China’s AI Frontlines ” with Scott Kennedy, trustee chair in Chinese Business and Economics at the Center for Strategic & International Studies, and Paul Triolo, CCA honorary senior fellow on technology, moderated by Lizzi.

3. Beijing Defends AI Export Controls Amid Push for OpennessWhat Happened: Yuyuan Tantian, a state-affiliated media account that often serves as a conduit for Beijing's trade-policy messaging, published a commentary pushing back against claims that China's proposed AI export controls set forth in Announcement No. 48 (October 16, 2026) signal a retreat from technological openness. The proposal would add 23 AI technologies, including technologies related to large language models and AI agents, to China's export control regime. The commentary defended MOFCOM's approach as pro-open-source while drawing clearer regulatory boundaries around sensitive technology transfers that involve data security, end-use concerns, and compute infrastructure. More importantly, it portrayed China’s emerging export control regime as nondiscriminatory and based on objective metrics, in contrast to the U.S. approach, and argued that this could strengthen China's role in shaping global AI governance rules. While the proposed rules also cover biotechnology and solar panel technologies, the commentary did not address these sectors.

Why It Matters: Open-weight models are China's main competitive edge in AI and underpin its claim to leadership in global AI governance. Any perception that Beijing is restricting their international release risks undermining both. The omission of biotechnology may be equally deliberate. As biotech export controls emerge as a potential bargaining chip in upcoming U.S.-China trade talks, publicly defending or emphasizing these restrictions could limit MOFCOM's flexibility to negotiate over them. MOFCOM's detailed carve-outs, reinforced by Yuyuan Tantian's commentary, also reflect an underlying policy tension between promoting China's AI development and controlling sensitive technology transfers. While the NDRC coordinates China's broader AI development agenda, MOFCOM's authority over technology exports gives it a regulatory role in overseeing frontier model developers, even as the State Council pushes to accelerate AI development.

By Huiyan Li and Shengyu Wang, Research Assistants, Center for China Analysis

Learn More: Read " https://click.e.asiasociety.org/?qs=ABt7ImQiOjUwNDUsInQiOjE3NTY5LCJzIjoxMH0ACgAAAAAF37IdlPGxahDFCrzMqzDiAAFz4_5jvhd6cbSXe8KwOcuSre-_1ZwgSSFjVuaACkr-xTbl6msCxyXU2O9sjCYk0QL3pKWfdGp7qXSr A Pragmatic Roadmap for the Bureaucratic Complexities of the Coming U.S.-China AI Safety Dialog " by Honorary Senior Fellows Paul Triolo and Alvin W. Graylin, on https://click.e.asiasociety.org/?qs=ABt7ImQiOjUwNDUsInQiOjE3NTY5LCJzIjoxMH0ACgAAAAAF37IdlPJ85gpRbccgnDqf18_ka5kajIXwt752E9c9AABOgITRHx9IJI5T6ZdBHIjw5SBWckix5vZvh_PCYzCp1J838ub2BF8nVOYc CCA’s Substack .

4. Kim Jong Un’s Dandong Visit Highlights Renewed China–DPRK EngagementWhat Happened: On October 24, Kim Jong Un visited the border city of Dandong, one of China’s most important gateways for trade and commerce with North Korea. Kim drove across the newly opened New Yalu River Bridge and was escorted throughout the visit by Wang Huning, China’s fourth-ranking leader, rather than by Xi Jinping himself. During Kim’s stay, he laid a flower basket at the monument outside the Memorial Hall of the War to Resist U.S. Aggression and Aid Korea to mark the 76th anniversary of China’s entry into the Korean War. He also visited the Dandong Huanghai Automobile plant, whose origins date back to a military vehicle repair factory established in 1951 to support Chinese operations during the Korean War.

Why It Matters: Bilateral ties between Beijing and Pyongyang have gained momentum since Kim Jong Un visited Beijing in September 2025 for a military parade marking the 80th anniversary of Japan’s defeat in World War II. Xi Jinping then visited North Korea in June, his first trip there in seven years. With Pyongyang tilting heavily toward Russia during the war in Ukraine, Beijing has moved to rebuild its influence over North Korea. Wang Huning's prominent role during Kim's visit, following his own visit to North Korea in July, underscores his importance as a senior intermediary in China-DPRK relations. Finally, the new bridge stands as the centerpiece for border development. Although Beijing and Pyongyang touted the bridge as a catalyst for trade and regional growth, its opening had been delayed for 12 years amid unfinished infrastructure on the North Korean side and political frictions. Kim’s decision to cross it by motorcade points to the bridge’s symbolic importance for renewed bilateral relations.

By Wei-Hao Ho, Intern, and Lyle Morris, Senior Fellow on Foreign Policy and National Security, Center for China Analysis

Learn More: For more on China's perspectives on North Korea and the Korean War, see this week’s https://click.e.asiasociety.org/?qs=ABt7ImQiOjUwNDUsInQiOjE3NTY5LCJzIjoxMH0ACgAAAAAF37IdlPMmQE4bswyIEyld-WVScJ79cHCzh1Jt-vW8iX7Iay76DwbpA4iNEV3QEyu2xr8B2L3f1nBERu8Zh9lTeGZRnFQ78F3qRWK4 special issue of PLA Watch .

5. China's Disaster Losses Fall as Human Toll RisesWhat Happened: On October 22, China’s Ministry of Emergency Management announced that in the first nine months of 2026, China recorded 199.36 billion yuan ($29.9 billion) in direct losses from natural disasters, driven primarily by severe typhoons, flooding, and landslides. Compared to the same period last year, losses were down 8% from 217.65 billion yuan. The ministry noted that from January to September 2026, more than 5 million people were forced to relocate or seek emergency shelter ⁠across the country, while 1,162 people were reported dead or missing due to natural disasters, up from 742 a ⁠year earlier.

Why It Matters: The latest figures underscore the importance of integrating climate adaptation, resilience, and disaster risk reduction into China’s policy and development planning. While direct economic losses declined year-on-year, the increase in deaths and missing persons, alongside substantial economic costs, highlights the need to sustain efforts to manage climate impacts. With geological hazards, including landslides and mudslides, accounting for the highest proportion of deaths and missing persons, and typhoons driving the greatest financial losses, policy efforts will need to address a range of hazards through targeted measures. Strengthening early warning systems, climate-resilient infrastructure, and targeted adaptation investment will be critical to reducing future losses and protecting vulnerable communities.

By Taylah Bland, Fellow on Climate and the Environment, Center for China Analysis (@Taylahbland)

Learn More: Read “ https://click.e.asiasociety.org/?qs=ABt7ImQiOjUwNDUsInQiOjE3NTY5LCJzIjoxMH0ACgAAAAAF37IdlPSnpGW87KoLymYdXnZ6ziQUeBCfPiRTKMBlyxbzq-SY1OHIXGyNSJbLQqTLVy4AtxbPMes1kc1h1w-ZvGyKM-9TEjsPXiHi While China Prepares for a Climate-Disrupted Future, the U.S. Is Taking a Different Path ” by Taylah.

Bill Kristol & Andrew Egger - The Bulwark

NEWSLETTER · 30 Oct 2026, 14:32

The Top Campaign Issue of the Decade Has Vanished

View this post on the web at https://www.thebulwark.com/p/the-top-campaign-issue-of-the-decade-abortion-midterms-ramaswamy-nuclear-power-data-centers

We’re nearly to the climax of Spooky Season: The midterms are just four days away. Bulwark+ members can join us on Sunday at 8:30 p.m. ET for a livestream on all things midterms—we’ll give you the state of play, our best predictions, and take your questions! Happy Friday. Where’d Roe Go? by Andrew Egger In 2022, the Supreme Court struck down Roe v. Wade, turned abortion access back into a state-level issue, and seemingly ushered in a new era of abortion-centric politics. For years, the issue was hotly contested: Red states passed laws slapping new abortion restrictions in place, while voters in many blue and red states passed ballot referendums rolling those restrictions back. In 2022 and especially 2024, Democrats spent heavily messaging the issue: Joe Biden and Kamala Harris campaigned in front of a “RESTORE ROE” banner while trying to stave off the comeback of a president who had embraced the label “the most pro-life president in American history.” Today, however, abortion politics is an afterthought. A few states [ https://substack.com/redirect/9287688f-b918-4195-94d5-94058d359b59?j=eyJ1IjoiNnhha3A0In0.Jik053lvvE-Ofpv9cpN9G_-RUPbW_JKjUogath2oHFk ] have abortion-policy ballot measures on the ballot this year, and the issue has come up in a few races: In Maine, Troy Jackson has repeatedly attacked Sen. Susan Collins for her votes to confirm the Supreme Court justices who ultimately struck down Roe, while Collins has pointed out that Jackson opposed abortion early in his legislative career. By and large, though, the issue has vanished from candidates’ stump speeches and from the airwaves. This summer, AdImpact estimated [ https://substack.com/redirect/04d4503d-fb2d-48f2-8160-c04860406942?j=eyJ1IjoiNnhha3A0In0.Jik053lvvE-Ofpv9cpN9G_-RUPbW_JKjUogath2oHFk ] that Democrats were buying ads mentioning abortion four times less than in the same period in 2024. Adsmom, a platform that tracks digital ad spending on Meta and Google, found [ https://substack.com/redirect/31dee6c9-64f9-42e9-9f29-86ec21268a85?j=eyJ1IjoiNnhha3A0In0.Jik053lvvE-Ofpv9cpN9G_-RUPbW_JKjUogath2oHFk ] that political spending on abortion ads in October came fifteenth among 26 different topics, below issues like data centers and the climate. It’s common for one party or the other to inch away from a given issue as it becomes clear the opposition has the more popular position. What’s strange today is that both parties have dropped abortion messaging—for totally different reasons. Just a few days left until the midterms. Don’t miss out on everything The Bulwark has to offer. Join us. For Democrats, it’s mostly a matter of other issues crowding abortion out. In 2022 and 2024, when Democrats held the presidency, it was Republicans who were making the kick-the-bums-out cost-of-living message. Today, amid growing anger about rising prices thanks to tariffs and the Iran war, Democrats seem to see every moment not spent uttering the word “affordability” as a moment wasted. Cost of living, Democratic strategist David Axelrod told The Bulwark, is “an urgent issue that unifies the largest number of people, so it makes sense that other issues, such as abortion, are playing a lesser role.” Meanwhile, as Trump has become deeply unpopular and Democrats have pushed the political map deep into red states, they’re suddenly competing for the votes of plenty of Americans who may not fully agree with them on abortion politics. Here, too, it makes more sense to stick to the grand unifying theme of affordability. “I think the reproductive freedom conversation—although very important—is a very specialized and nuanced conversation,” said Antjuan Seawright, a Democratic strategist in South Carolina. “You don’t think about that when you’re going to pay your light bill.” All that makes sense. But it also makes it somewhat stranger that Republicans, playing defense in so many red states, aren’t turning to abortion politics to try to rally the troops. The GOP’s midterm strategy has been to give up independents for lost and spend all their time and energy juicing base turnout—so where’s the rally-round messaging for pro-lifers? One major factor is that Trump and his allies, having seen abortion-access initiatives pass even in red states in 2022 and 2024, are no longer confident that their base actually finds abortion politics particularly motivating. While abortion remains an animating issue for highly religious voters, most social conservatives seem to have executed a Trump-led pivot toward other culture-war issues—most notably rabble-rousing about trans people—without much complaining. “The pro-life movement died when Trump said it was a state issue. It’s not part of the Republican coalition right now. It’s a side issue. This is tragic, obviously, but it’s the truth.” –A social-conservative operative Even for the highly anti-abortion conservatives that remain, Republicans today would just as soon have them thinking about something else. That’s because, among the major issue groups of the GOP coalition, they alone have not received the major executive action they’ve sought: The “most pro-life president in history” has not changed FDA regulations to forbid sending the abortion drug mifepristone through the mail. The administration has been slow-walking a long-promised review on the subject, plainly trying to get to the far side of the midterms before making any decisions. Pro-life groups haven’t been impressed. “The FDA’s court-ordered update on its abortion-drug study is alarming,” Susan B. Anthony Pro-Life America President Marjorie Dannenfelser said this month [ https://substack.com/redirect/4bfa7caf-06f3-47cd-981b-a2b756b0becd?j=eyJ1IjoiNnhha3A0In0.Jik053lvvE-Ofpv9cpN9G_-RUPbW_JKjUogath2oHFk ]. “Instead of the promised gold-standard study, the FDA is trying to peddle fool’s gold.” Even though nobody seems to want to talk about it right now, abortion politics isn’t going away for good. If Democrats retake a trifecta in 2028, they will likely face major base pressure to re-codify Roe in federal law. And when the midterms are past, the FDA will be out of excuses: Sooner or later, it will have to rule on mifepristone, and some coalition of Americans isn’t going to like it when they do. Whatever abortion-politics hiatus we’re on now, it isn’t going to last. Atomic Ramaswamy by Jim Swift Last spring, during the early stages of his party’s primary, Ohio Republican gubernatorial candidate Vivek Ramaswamy declared himself “strongly in favor” of Ohio’s data center boom. But that boom has since become a political liability. A September poll found that among likely voters, three-quarters [ https://substack.com/redirect/67d6ad5b-41a5-49e8-8110-741937683357?j=eyJ1IjoiNnhha3A0In0.Jik053lvvE-Ofpv9cpN9G_-RUPbW_JKjUogath2oHFk ] oppose having a data center built in their community, and 78 percent support a temporary ban on new construction. Democratic candidate Amy Acton is making sure voters remember her opponent’s prior enthusiasm. Her “Dirty Rich” ad [ https://substack.com/redirect/b09dbe11-f180-4cd3-8a3d-431f10614c8e?j=eyJ1IjoiNnhha3A0In0.Jik053lvvE-Ofpv9cpN9G_-RUPbW_JKjUogath2oHFk ] juxtaposes Ramaswamy bragging about his wealth with footage of him declaring himself pro-boom, loudly implying the guy is getting rich off developments that are driving up [ https://substack.com/redirect/a2ed68dd-bc15-4c5c-8e15-b7a5553d1090?j=eyJ1IjoiNnhha3A0In0.Jik053lvvE-Ofpv9cpN9G_-RUPbW_JKjUogath2oHFk ] electricity prices for normal Ohioans. Ramaswamy’s data center–related holdings are fairly well known [ https://substack.com/redirect/a2d00173-9da0-432d-a9b9-7e32ae097306?j=eyJ1IjoiNnhha3A0In0.Jik053lvvE-Ofpv9cpN9G_-RUPbW_JKjUogath2oHFk ]: He owns stock in companies that build and supply them, including Amazon, Microsoft, and Nvidia, and has other relevant investments in semiconductor companies and crypto. But what about the nuclear plants he’s been pitching [ https://substack.com/redirect/e5ca1513-83b2-4502-a4d8-505386b46faf?j=eyJ1IjoiNnhha3A0In0.Jik053lvvE-Ofpv9cpN9G_-RUPbW_JKjUogath2oHFk ] to power them? When it comes to the intersection of energy policy and private profit, the state’s voters carry a certain amount of residual trauma. Memories of the FirstEnergy bribes-for-a-bailout scheme [ https://substack.com/redirect/8d69a703-2660-4d98-91a2-473e35c16bd7?j=eyJ1IjoiNnhha3A0In0.Jik053lvvE-Ofpv9cpN9G_-RUPbW_JKjUogath2oHFk ] are still fresh. It was the biggest scandal in Ohio’s history, and the continuing aftershocks include the suicides [ https://substack.com/redirect/f1738f15-53f9-4fe8-8a50-79aa66b6037b?j=eyJ1IjoiNnhha3A0In0.Jik053lvvE-Ofpv9cpN9G_-RUPbW_JKjUogath2oHFk ] of two people who had been implicated in the malfeasance. Ramaswamy has said he would sell his data center–related stocks if elected; he claimed [ https://substack.com/redirect/10e2d5c1-26ce-44c5-91bf-851037be505f?j=eyJ1IjoiNnhha3A0In0.Jik053lvvE-Ofpv9cpN9G_-RUPbW_JKjUogath2oHFk ] they were just a “speck of dust” in his portfolio. Atlas Atomics, a nuclear startup Ramaswamy co-founded and backed [ https://substack.com/redirect/63247f58-47c0-4a15-9fcb-e9ef35b4e646?j=eyJ1IjoiNnhha3A0In0.Jik053lvvE-Ofpv9cpN9G_-RUPbW_JKjUogath2oHFk ], is probably a bit harder to brush off. Bloomberg reported [ https://substack.com/redirect/43e20461-2047-4b8c-b11c-da094c4977d2?j=eyJ1IjoiNnhha3A0In0.Jik053lvvE-Ofpv9cpN9G_-RUPbW_JKjUogath2oHFk ] earlier this month that Atlas had raised roughly $400 million at a valuation of about $1.9 billion, with MAGA-friendly Andreessen Horowitz participating. The size and value of Ramaswamy’s stake in the company are not publicly known. Maintaining his position, however, would likely create significant conflicts of interest for Ramaswamy should he succeed in his bid for the governor’s mansion. I have not been able to find any examples of the candidate publicly addressing his investment in Atlas or being asked to explain it during his campaign for governor. The Ramaswamy campaign did not respond to my request for comment. Ramaswamy has been somewhat freer in offering relevant comments elsewhere, however. In May, he delivered [ https://substack.com/redirect/92a2e352-3a8a-4ac5-bb08-40cc4f5ca4f3?j=eyJ1IjoiNnhha3A0In0.Jik053lvvE-Ofpv9cpN9G_-RUPbW_JKjUogath2oHFk ] a keynote at Utah’s Operation Gigawatt Summit in which he advocated for state and federal leaders to help win the PR battle over nuclear power and data centers. Within two weeks, Atlas was signing an MOU [ https://substack.com/redirect/de738276-1683-4215-874b-a93df61bb406?j=eyJ1IjoiNnhha3A0In0.Jik053lvvE-Ofpv9cpN9G_-RUPbW_JKjUogath2oHFk ] with the state’s Office of Energy Development to explore the prospect of building a roughly 20-megawatt test reactor and a 350-megawatt commercial reactor using advanced heavy-water technology in Utah; the non-binding agreement also raises the possibility of “state and local incentives.” (Read: tax breaks.) Ramaswamy has meanwhile professed opposition [ https://substack.com/redirect/2fabf2de-7d41-46a6-8714-85c7b21202b8?j=eyJ1IjoiNnhha3A0In0.Jik053lvvE-Ofpv9cpN9G_-RUPbW_JKjUogath2oHFk ] to tax breaks for development while on the campaign trail in Ohio. His position as a candidate back in the Midwest has morphed from cheerleading the data center boom to taking a much stricter line; he now insists that data-center developers generate their own power [ https://substack.com/redirect/025cbdad-7c29-4dab-adb3-fa76f06108a5?j=eyJ1IjoiNnhha3A0In0.Jik053lvvE-Ofpv9cpN9G_-RUPbW_JKjUogath2oHFk ] and cover nearby residents’ electricity costs (or power their homes directly, gratis, with surplus energy generated by the data centers’ own facilities). Nuclear energy is mentioned as an option in his proposal [ https://substack.com/redirect/e5a17e94-fb5f-4416-bdc6-5a7fa69a086d?j=eyJ1IjoiNnhha3A0In0.Jik053lvvE-Ofpv9cpN9G_-RUPbW_JKjUogath2oHFk ]. The governor would have substantial influence over the implementation of such a plan. All seven permanent voting positions on the Ohio Power Siting Board [ https://substack.com/redirect/4053de98-13af-4372-bcdc-888d90de5145?j=eyJ1IjoiNnhha3A0In0.Jik053lvvE-Ofpv9cpN9G_-RUPbW_JKjUogath2oHFk ] are held by gubernatorial appointees. That board reviews covered power-generation projects, though federal nuclear licensing remains separate. Promoting nuclear power is defensible, especially as a means of lowering carbon emissions. But retaining a financial interest in a nuclear developer while serving as governor facing major strains on your state’s power grid raises significant ethical questions. Would Ramaswamy sell his entire interest in Atlas before taking office? If he intends to keep it, Ohioans deserve to know how he would keep that investment from influencing the state’s energy decisions. FirstEnergy was bad enough the first time. [ https://substack.com/redirect/36bb3162-4fe1-45f8-b771-a36042f5cd7e?j=eyJ1IjoiNnhha3A0In0.Jik053lvvE-Ofpv9cpN9G_-RUPbW_JKjUogath2oHFk ] QUICK HITS WHAT’S RIGHT WITH KANSAS?: On Monday, the Kansas Republican Party announced that Donald Trump would be holding a rally for incumbent Sen. Roger Marshall in Salina, the prairie-town hub of central Kansas. Republicans feel the need to roll Trump out because the race is extremely competitive: Marshall is well known for his arrogance, poor constituent services, and miserly approach to medical debt, while the Democratic challenger, Methodist minister Adam Hamilton, is uniquely suited to the state despite being somewhat to the left of its median voter. The latest CNN/SSRS poll has Hamilton up by 4 points [ https://substack.com/redirect/36c23814-975c-4e8e-b097-73aa987eec41?j=eyJ1IjoiNnhha3A0In0.Jik053lvvE-Ofpv9cpN9G_-RUPbW_JKjUogath2oHFk ] in a state that hasn’t elected a Democratic senator since 1932. In a rush of excitement about landing the big guy, local Republicans spammed seemingly everyone with a 785 area code on Wednesday. But then yesterday, the White House abruptly cancelled the rally, opting [ https://substack.com/redirect/fb953df7-d323-4eae-a659-770ca7b3a14b?j=eyJ1IjoiNnhha3A0In0.Jik053lvvE-Ofpv9cpN9G_-RUPbW_JKjUogath2oHFk ] for a “shotgun” approach in which the president will hold twenty-odd “tele-rallies.” It seems like shuttling Trump to the middle of Kansas ate up too much of his time when there’s plenty of GOP desperation to go around. But here’s another theory: Maybe Trump’s presence would have helped Hamilton. Kansas may be dyed-in-the-wool Republican, but it’s also seen enough bad conservative governance to make it show some Midwestern common sense. After Gov. Sam Brownback’s failed taxation experiment, Kansans twice elected Democrat Laura Kelly to the governor’s office. In 2022, Kansans voted down a strict anti-abortion constitutional amendment by 18 points, and in this cycle repudiated an effort to increase partisan influence on state judges by a wide margin [ https://substack.com/redirect/2e97a90d-74ac-4ddc-ab5f-37a4fa10da10?j=eyJ1IjoiNnhha3A0In0.Jik053lvvE-Ofpv9cpN9G_-RUPbW_JKjUogath2oHFk ]. Perhaps Republican strategists realized that reminding rural Kansans who’s behind their high diesel prices, deportations of meatworkers, and job-killing tariffs was actually a pretty big risk. –Joshua Tait MORE JD LIES: We’ve written a couple times now about a shameless attempt to stoke anti-trans panic ahead of the midterms in Texas: inflammatory mailers addressed to the children of GOP voters that claimed to come from a Texas trans-rights group, but which, according to legal filings made public yesterday [ https://substack.com/redirect/4e196858-662e-488c-9fd6-43a7b3b9e975?j=eyJ1IjoiNnhha3A0In0.Jik053lvvE-Ofpv9cpN9G_-RUPbW_JKjUogath2oHFk ], were in fact sent by a state GOP group, the Republican Women’s Caucus Fund. This was far from the first indication the mailers were a GOP operation; last week, the group that sent them retained a lawyer who has previously represented both Ken Paxton and Elon Musk in high-profile matters. Someone, however, has mysteriously failed to keep up with these developments. Yesterday, at his rally in Texas, Vice President JD Vance managed to give the impression—although he avoided claiming it directly—that the mailers had come from James Talarico: At an earlier event, I was talking to a mom who told me—and I was shocked by it, we’re actually going to have the Department of Justice investigate—that her 11-year-old child got a letter encouraging that child to transition to a different gender. Now Talafreako calls the kind of person that would send that letter an honest and decent trans person that he’s fighting for. I call a person who would send a letter like that to an 11-year-old a pervert that deserves a prison sentence. Harsh words, but the Republican Women’s Caucus Fund probably has little to fear. Now that it’s obvious that the “pervert that deserves a prison sentence” was a local gang of GOP ratfuckers, we suspect that the DOJ will wrap up its “investigation” pretty quick. CHEAP SHOTS

Korean human rights ( https://b85a80bb.click.convertkit-mail.com/27uqggn90qaohgr0knlf3hggzrgvzbghvg8e/qvh8h7hd3ere3gilhk/aHR0cHM6Ly9lbi55bmEuY28ua3Ivdmlldy9BRU4yMDI2MTAyOTAwNzIwMDMxNQ== )Yonhap News ( https://b85a80bb.click.convertkit-mail.com/27uqggn90qaohgr0knlf3hggzrgvzbghvg8e/qvh8h7hd3ere3gilhk/aHR0cHM6Ly9lbi55bmEuY28ua3Ivdmlldy9BRU4yMDI2MTAyOTAwNzIwMDMxNQ== )The unification ministry is in talks with relevant government agencies on whether to co-sponsor this year's U.N General Assembly resolution on North Korean human rights, a ministry official said Thursday. "It is difficult to comment on the specifics at this stage," the official told reporters, adding that the government's position will be finalized "soon." Leisure trips of this kind were once seen as a pastime reserved for the country’s wealthiest traders, but now even women with regular jobs are carving out a day or two in autumn 2026 for outings. “More women have been going on outings in small groups now that the cool autumn weather has arrived,” a source in North Hamgyong province told Daily NK on Thursday. “People used to think only rich donju went on trips like this. Now they see it as something you can do if your life has even a little breathing room.”

EUROPE NEEDS BETTER GOVERNANCE, NOT A NEW CONSTITUTION

“There is a persistent temptation in Brussels to look for an institutional solution to every political problem. If the European Union is not delivering, the argument goes, it must be because its institutions are inadequate. Reform the institutions and the problems will disappear. Unfortunately, things are not so simple.”

Read the full editorial

Can Europe confront its digital challenges? |

EuropeChats with Seamus Allen

In this episode of EuropeChats, TEPSA Secretary-General Jim Cloos sat down with Seamus Allen, Senior Researcher at the Institute of International and European Affairs, to discuss Europe’s digital future, competitiveness, and the rise of artificial intelligence.

And why should you care about Bulgaria’s future? TEPSA Project Manager, Allegra Wirmer, tells us more in this new episode of EU Democracy Unpacked.

A debate is raging in Europe about the European Green Deal. Launched with broad political support in 2019, it shaped the mandate of the first von der Leyen Commission. Seven years later, that consensus has eroded.

In our latest TEPSA Experts’ Debrief, Secretary-General Jim Cloos asked 10 experts to provide their assessment of the EU’s climate policies and their ideas on the way ahead. How can we reconcile the Green Deal with the EU’s quest for competitiveness?

Read the Debrief here

“Strategic autonomy will not be built through any one action, but through a long-term, sustained vision to act together in the areas where acting alone demonstrates the greatest vulnerability.”

In our latest TEPSA Brief, Alec Vida, Giulia Tagliento, Karlien Deleu, and Benjamin Gambs identify pathways and priorities to strengthen the EU, drawing on lessons learned from a student-led workshop held by the Centre international de formation européenne (CIFE) with TEPSA.

The event is co-organised with the Institute of International Relations and Political Science of Vilnius University (IIRPS) to discuss the challenges and priorities of the upcoming Lithuanian Presidency of the Council of the EU. Updates from the TEPSA Secretariat

On 8 October 2026, experts from the TEPSA Network met in Chișinău to present the TEPSA book “Security, Defence and the Future of Europe: Views from the Capitals” at the Moldova Security Forum 2026.

Book authors Tony Lawrence (ICDS), Funda Tekin (IEP), Marion Messmer (Chatham House) and Iulian Groza (IPRE) presented their countries' perspectives on the future of European defence.

The ChangingTIDE Changemakers programme equips young citizens with the skills to do exactly that. On 16 October, 20 Europeans met in Brussels to learn about disinformation, practise building awareness-raising campaigns and gain media literacy skills.

ChinaTalk

1 item in this evidence packet.

NEWSLETTER · 30 Oct 2026, 11:03

Xi vs Trump on Corruption

View this post on the web at https://www.chinatalk.media/p/make-corruption-cringe-again

In February, Eric and Donald Jr. sat down with a reporter [ https://substack.com/redirect/c7f80511-fcee-4c6c-a586-dc51ee567589?j=eyJ1IjoiNnhha3A0In0.Jik053lvvE-Ofpv9cpN9G_-RUPbW_JKjUogath2oHFk ] to explain why Abu Dhabi paid $500m for a stake in World Liberty Financial. Don Jr. said, “They tried all this nonsense the first time around. Frankly, it’s gotten old…They created this monster.” The monster of political corruption has been with us since the founding. But since the Progressive Eva, it has been cut with at least a tinge of embarrassment. The Trumps instead have done it in broad daylight and tried to make it a flex. China’s political system is riddled with corruption on an entirely different scale. The skims in China happen up and down the system, and making a billion dollars while in office is nothing new [ https://substack.com/redirect/0d4d6254-4b54-4330-bf18-97e199251a69?j=eyJ1IjoiNnhha3A0In0.Jik053lvvE-Ofpv9cpN9G_-RUPbW_JKjUogath2oHFk ] for senior Chinese leaders. But Xi understands what this costs the Party. In his first Politburo speech as General Secretary, he warned that corruption, left to spread, would “doom the Party and the state.” Xi was horrified by seeing youth with bloodlines just as red as his crash foreign sports cars in the streets of Beijing. He views the princeling inheritance as a responsibility to serve, not a license to fuckboy. As Kamil Galeev writes [ https://substack.com/redirect/090ab9db-944e-4726-a736-ea38037be828?j=eyJ1IjoiNnhha3A0In0.Jik053lvvE-Ofpv9cpN9G_-RUPbW_JKjUogath2oHFk ], Xi aims to extirpate “the alien, unfamiliar, threatening bandit entrepreneurial culture — not even from the country but rather, from the national elites.” Xi is a believer, but Trump is a businessman. After Trump brought down the guardrails with a compliant SCOTUS and Congress, the presidential family has been sloppily revenue-maximizing ever since. Xi’s anti-corruption campaign hasn’t liquidated corruption in China, which still lubricates a much higher percentage of economic activity than similar behavior does in America. But Xi has succeeded in making it dangerous. Making outside money in Xi’s China runs the risk of execution — far different stakes than embarrassing ProPublica articles revealing crimes that get pardoned away. At least Chinese corruption built things. Chinese officials got paid and promoted for delivering growth, so the bribes came attached to airports, rail lines, chip fabs and subsidized EV plants. Nobody today is flying out of the new terminal a Binance pardon made happen. What legacies will Trump leave that will truly outlast him? If President Ossoff plants zucchini on the helipad and turns the ballroom into a preschool, how will Trump have really left his mark? Three that are baked in include depleted munitions stockpiles, a loss of trust from allies, and the millions of aspiring immigrants who ended up studying or living somewhere else. But it’s still an open question whether Trump-style corruption in the White House will outlast him. I remain hopeful it won’t! Even if prosecutors in the U.S. can barely punish corruption, voters still can. It took a while, but Trump’s corruption has broken through to the public. Corruption now tops voters’ concerns in Fox News’s own polling, and Democrats are closing the midterms on it. Trump and his courtiers may never pay for what they’ve done; the legislators that sat back as this happened will. The next president will either have run a campaign against corruption or been forced to run away from it. For all their faults, Newsom, Ossoff and AOC don’t seem like the type to launch a memecoin. Neither Vance nor Rubio gives off particularly venal energy. Plus, their children will be too young to do deals with Putin. Meanwhile in China, corruption is too structural for even a leader as motivated as Xi to really get a lid on. He’s working against five decades of corrupt muscle memory and a system that incentivizes cadres old and new to use money to get things done, or get theirs. Without a free press or voters sick of the status quo, Xi can only police the Party with the Party. And the judicial system can never be independent because the Party believes it has to preserve its right to decide cases as it sees fit. 14 years into the job, Xi still can’t get graft off the agenda. This week, Xi devoted the latest plenum to yet again cleaning house. Its communique [ https://substack.com/redirect/5c2cf410-5e58-4825-b086-0585e7e46f03?j=eyJ1IjoiNnhha3A0In0.Jik053lvvE-Ofpv9cpN9G_-RUPbW_JKjUogath2oHFk ] declared the Party’s lax self-policing “addressed at the root,” then confirmed 33 more expulsions, including Liu Jianchao, who once ran Xi’s global manhunt for corrupt officials. Expect a few of the 33 in January’s installment of the state TV documentary where fallen officials confess on camera, because the Party still thinks the public needs to see corruption punished. Trump’s sons got their TV moment in February, telling CNBC that the criticism had gotten old and that the banks created this monster. On November 3, the people get to say whether they agree.

A sugar-rush economy leaves a sour taste

View this post on the web at https://www.geopoliticaldispatch.com/p/a-sugar-rush-economy-leaves-a-sour

Hello, Here are the five things you need to know today: UNITED STATES. GDP comes in hot but may soon go off. VENEZUELA. A Trump-backed election is as fishy as it sounds. IRAN. Markets start to price in near-term escalation. FRANCE. The ECB’s president quits to speak her mind. INDIA. The cockroaches move back onto the streets. You’re reading one of today’s five analyses. Subscribe to read all five, every weekday. UNITED STATES. Artificial ingredients GDP comes in hot but may soon go off. Third-quarter GDP rose 3.4% annualised, with strong AI-related investment offsetting higher oil prices and low consumer confidence. Weekly jobless claims came in at a 57-year low. Nasdaq futures, however, tanked on tech earnings upsets. INTELLIGENCE. Artificial Intelligence (or Super Intelligence to Donald Trump) has rescued the US from recession, but may also send it there, if stock prices are an omen. Vast spending on data centres is yet to turn a profit. Alphabet’s negative cashflow saw its shares fall 12.6%, wiping out $500 billion in value (the GDP of Denmark). Amazon and Intel fell 7% and 23% in aftermarket trading. Federal debt, meanwhile, has hit 100% of GDP, the highest level since WWII. FOR BUSINESS. While GDP is a lagging indicator, the Nasdaq is down 5.13% since its all-time high on 6 October. This isn’t great for Anthropic’s November IPO, but spare a thought for China’s tech sector. The ChiNext index is down 38% from its all-time high on 13 May, with unprofitability and circular financing also causing investor concern. At least its leading labs, Moonshot and Z.AI, can now claim to have bested Anthropic’s Fable 5 in key performance benchmarks. VENEZUELA. Democracy in the Americas A Trump-backed election is as fishy as it sounds. Opposition leader Maria Corina Machado said Washington had proposed Caracas hold elections in 2027 but did not mention Trump's suggestion of making Venezuela the 51st state. President Delcy Rodriguez flagged state asset sales...

By David Bauer • 30 Oct 2026

View in browser [https://newsletter.weeklyfilet.com/r/a8c2d929?m=4456d7d4-72b3-4c6c-abdb-0791c4934aed]

Welcome, curious minds

This is David [https://newsletter.weeklyfilet.com/r/4690e427?m=4456d7d4-72b3-4c6c-abdb-0791c4934aed], your ever so ambitious web guide, and you're reading the Weekly Filet [https://newsletter.weeklyfilet.com/r/a7f7729a?m=4456d7d4-72b3-4c6c-abdb-0791c4934aed], the newsletter for curious minds who love it when something makes them go «Huh, I never thought of it this way!».

Last week, I asked you to tell me why you like reading the newsletter. I got so many heartwarming answers, thank you! One of you told me they like how the newsletter «puts ideas in my head for the weekend», which helps keep work-related thoughts away until Monday. I had never thought of it that way – glad the Weekly Filet works like this.

1. How to be more ambitious: A tutorial for humans with models [https://newsletter.weeklyfilet.com/r/8e9012f9?m=4456d7d4-72b3-4c6c-abdb-0791c4934aed]

Large language models are already much more powerful than most of us realise. And I don't mean this in the apocalypse-is-imminent way, but in that they can be way more valuable to each of us when we know how to use them. In short, Felix Rieseberg writes in this article, we need to be «more ambitious»: «In a world where our abilities make leaps every couple of weeks, we need techniques that allow us to do things we didn’t really consider or believe possible.»

How to be more ambitious: A tutorial for humans with models I work on Claude. It's an interesting experience: Every day I learn about yet another feat accomplished with or by Clau…

Felix Rieseberg •Felix Rieseberg

[https://newsletter.weeklyfilet.com/r/2b0da840?m=4456d7d4-72b3-4c6c-abdb-0791c4934aed]

(The author also has one of the best About Me [https://newsletter.weeklyfilet.com/r/de347ee3?m=4456d7d4-72b3-4c6c-abdb-0791c4934aed] pages I've ever seen, so make sure to check that out as well).

2. Norrsken 100 [https://newsletter.weeklyfilet.com/r/4d08f3b7?m=4456d7d4-72b3-4c6c-abdb-0791c4934aed]

To address huge challenges like the climate crisis, we should absolutely invest in technology that might help. However, we must not hope for technology to solve all our problems so we can just keep going. I call this approach technologieoffen, nicht technologiebesoffen (sorry, it doesn't really translate well). If you can keep that at the back of your mind, this is an interesting list to explore: «The hundred most promising early-stage companies solving hard problems at scale, by outperforming the legacy models that created them.»

3. How to read your house [https://newsletter.weeklyfilet.com/r/4934f06c?m=4456d7d4-72b3-4c6c-abdb-0791c4934aed]

«Our houses, while valid maps of who we are, also contain a lot of evidence about the people we expected to be.» Wonderful short essay on noticing change and making adjustments.

How to read your house - #224 The floorplan, the map, and our better selves.

Shangrilogs •Kelton Wright

[https://newsletter.weeklyfilet.com/r/e8056597?m=4456d7d4-72b3-4c6c-abdb-0791c4934aed]

4. Eurosclerosis or US Decline? [https://newsletter.weeklyfilet.com/r/2cb650dc?m=4456d7d4-72b3-4c6c-abdb-0791c4934aed]

A sharp analysis and the case for looking more closely. A common headline is that over the past decade, Europe's economy has fallen behind the US economy, going from «90 percent US GDP to 65 percent». The numbers are correct, but it gets interesting when we ask what we're actually measuring (what if we looked at GDPPPP instead?). And what we're optimising for («The difference is therefore not due to a labour market failure, but instead the result of the US simply choosing to work more and Europe consistently choosing to convert productivity gains into leisure time.»)

Dominik Leusder | Eurosclerosis or US Decline? The debate about US and European competitiveness illustrates how narratives of economic decline and triumphalism tend t…

Phenomenal World •Dominik Leusder

[https://newsletter.weeklyfilet.com/r/be5c582f?m=4456d7d4-72b3-4c6c-abdb-0791c4934aed]

5. I Teach at a Business School. It’s Time to Shut Them Down. [https://newsletter.weeklyfilet.com/r/5e2bde0b?m=4456d7d4-72b3-4c6c-abdb-0791c4934aed]

No punches pulled in this opinion piece: business schools cannot be fixed, because they aren't broken. They are «highly efficient machines doing exactly what they were designed to do [...] training the future leaders who will design and defend the structures driving our overlapping global crises.» So they need to be abolished.
